## MICRO-F-002
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
basis: Ch. 12 — Viral Replication {#ch12-6}
evidence: The lysogenic cycle integrates phage DNA into host DNA, and the host cell "copies its own DNA and the virus DNA... passing virus DNA to all its offspring" — viral genes replicate as part of the host's cycle, not independently of it.
seen: model 1, model 3, model 4, model 5, model 6

Each of the following statements concerning lysogeny is correct EXCEPT?
a) Viral DNA is integrated into bacterial DNA
b) Viral genes replicate independently of bacterial genes
c) Viral genes responsible for lysis are repressed
d) Some lysogenic bacteriophage encode toxins that cause human disease

## MICRO-F-004
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single

note: Page also carries a hand-drawn X over (a) and a check mark on (c). Moodle's own filled radio already selected (c), so per job-a-extract.md the hand marks are ignored as annotation, not treated as a separate claim — they agree with the Moodle state anyway, so nothing is lost either way.

The O antigen is used to identified the different members of Enterobacteriaceae group,
this antigen belongs to?
a) Flagella

a) Salmonella typhi
b) Campylobacter jejuni

## MICRO-F-007
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: c
basis: Ch. 12 — Viral Replication {#ch12-3}
evidence: Negative-polarity, segmented RNA viruses (the described 8-segment virus) require a virion polymerase and their genome is not itself infectious — per the RNA features table, "Segmented | - | Yes [polymerase] | Transcription | No [infective genome]." Purified RNA extracted from such a particle is therefore not infectious.
seen: model 1, model 3, model 6

The nucleic acid of a certain virus was found to be composed of eight pieces of
single-stranded negative polarity RNA. Which one of the following statements is not
correct?
a) Different proteins are encoded by each segment of the viral genome
b) The virus particle have to contain a virus-encoded enzyme that can copy and
   replicate the genome
c) Purified RNA extracted from the virus particle is infectious
d) This kind of virus may undergo genome rearmament or reassortment in presence of
   similar viral segments from other animal related viruses..

## MICRO-F-009
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: a
basis: Ch. 7 — Pathogenic Mechanisms {#ch07-7}
evidence: "Cell wall proteins of Gram-positive cocci... protein A of S. aureus... protein A binds IgG and prevents complement activation," listed directly under the chapter's virulence-factors section.
note: Disputed source (model 1: "All the options are correct", model 5: "Protein A that binds to the Fc portion of IgG"). The source supports the model 5 claim. Model 1's claim fails on its own logic too: option (d), "inhibits glycine release," is tetanus toxin's mechanism (Ch.7: "Tetanus toxin... prevents release of... glycine"), not S. aureus's — so "all options correct" cannot hold regardless of the dispute.
seen: model 1, model 4, model 5

Which one of the following is considered a virulence factor for Staphylococcus aureus?
a) Protein A that binds to the Fc portion of IgG
b) A heat stable exotoxin.
c) All the options are correct.
d) A heat-labile toxin that inhibits glycine release in neuron synapsis.

## MICRO-F-048
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: c
basis: Ch. 8 — Transmission and Spread of Microorganisms {#ch08-5}
evidence: The chapter's vertical-transmission table confirms HIV as a vertically transmitted pathogen and establishes transplacental, birth-canal, and breast-milk as the three recognized routes. The table lists each pathogen once, under its single most clinically prominent route — that structure doesn't amount to a claim that HIV or Rubella cross by no other route, so a table entry elsewhere isn't a contradiction of transplacental transmission here.
note: The table itself lists HIV under "within birth canal" and doesn't mention Rubella at all — on a narrow reading this looks unsupported or even contradicted. Standard teaching resolves it: Rubella is a classic TORCH-group transplacental infection, and HIV transmits in utero as well as intrapartum — the table is organized by predominant route per pathogen, not an exhaustive per-route listing, so it doesn't exclude the transplacental route for either organism. Recorded per job-c-verify.md's divergence rule: decided from the source's applicable content, with the apparent mismatch against the table's single-route categorization kept visible rather than dropped.

The following pathogens are transmitted trans placentally?
a) Escherichia coli, Neisseria gonorrhoeae
b) Herpes simplex virus type 1 and 2
c) HIV, Rubella
d) Staphylococcus aureus, Group B streptococci

basis: Ch. 7 — Pathogenic Mechanisms {#ch07-8}; Ch. 2 — Bacterial Cell — Structure and Function {#ch02-1}
evidence: Cholera's enterotoxin is described as "increasing cyclic AMP within the enterocyte, resulting in watery diarrhea," and Vibrio is listed among the "comma-shaped" (curved) Gram-negative rods.
seen: model 3, model 4, model 6

A 40-year-old man who recently visited India admitted to the emergency department
with profuse watery diarrhea with mucus, and severe dehydration. Which of the
following correctly describes the causal agent?
a) Gram-negative curved rod; toxin that inhibits protein synthesis
b) Gram-negative curved rods; toxin that increases cAMP.
c) Gram-negative rod; toxin that inhibits protein synthesis
d) All the mentioned could be the causative agent

## MICRO-F-087
status: external
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
basis: external — not covered in course material
evidence: Mannitol salt agar does not appear in Ch.4's agar table. Standard teaching: it is selective (high NaCl inhibits most bacteria except Staphylococcus) and differential (mannitol fermenters like S. aureus turn it yellow) at once.
seen: model 3, model 4, model 6

Mannitol salt agar is considered as?
a) Differential medium
b) Enrichment medium
c) Selective medium
d) Both selective and differential

## MICRO-F-088
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
basis: Ch. 14 — Antiviral Agents {#ch14-4}
evidence: Acyclovir's cell selectivity is explained through intracellular activation — "CMV is resistant to acyclovir because it does not encode a thymidine kinase, or its thymidine kinase is unable to phosphorylate the drug" — not through binding a surface receptor unique to infected cells. The statement is False.
seen: model 3, model 4

Selective action of Acyclovir in herpes simplex virus (HSV)-infected cells is
attributed to the specific binding of Acyclovir to viral receptors only expressed
on the surface of the HSV-infected cells.
a) True
b) False

## MICRO-F-089
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: c
basis: Ch. 10 — Sterilization and Disinfection {#ch10-4}
evidence: Iodine is described as killing by inactivating sulfhydryl-containing enzymes and binding tyrosine residues — thymine dimer formation is instead UV radiation's mechanism (Ch.10-5). The other three statements (autoclave at 121°C, pasteurization not sterilizing the milk, 70% alcohol outperforming 100%) all match the source directly.
seen: model 4, model 6

Which of the following statements is not correct regarding bacterial killing?
a) An autoclave uses steam under pressure to reach the killing temperature of 121°C.
b) Spores and some pathogens survive milk pasteurization process.
c) Iodine kills by the formation of thymine dimers in bacterial DNA.
d) A 70% ethanol kills more effectively than 100% ethanol.

## MICRO-F-090
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
basis: Ch. 2 — Bacterial Cell — Structure and Function {#ch02-9}
evidence: Spores require "steam heating under pressure (autoclaving) at 121°C for at least 15 minutes... to ensure sterility," precisely because boiling and ordinary chemical disinfectants fail against them — autoclaving is the method the source describes as effective.
seen: model 4, model 6

Bacterial spores are sensitive to?
a) None of them
b) Autoclaving
c) alcohol 70%
d) UV radiation
e) Boiling

## MICRO-F-091
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
basis: Ch. 7 — Pathogenic Mechanisms {#ch07-8}
evidence: "Botulinum toxin... blocks acetylcholine release at the synapse." Tetanus toxin instead blocks the inhibitory neurotransmitter glycine, not acetylcholine.
note: Near-duplicate theme of MICRO-F-052 (both about the toxin/organism that inhibits acetylcholine release at the neuromuscular junction) but this stem asks for the toxin itself rather than the producing bacterium, and the option set is toxin names rather than genus names — kept separate rather than merged.
seen: model 4

Which of the following toxins most likely inhibits the release of acetylcholine at
the neuromuscular junction?
a) Tetanus toxin
b) Cholera toxin
c) Diphtheria toxin
d) Botulinum toxin

## MICRO-F-092
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
basis: Ch. 11 — Viral Structure and Classification {#ch11-4}
evidence: All three component statements match the source: serotype is based on surface antigens, meaning capsid proteins for naked viruses and envelope proteins for enveloped viruses; and "some viruses have multiple serotypes" is stated explicitly (influenza, HIV, hepatitis C).
seen: model 4, model 6

Which of the following statements is correct concerning viral serotypes?
a) Viral serotype is usually determined by the outer capsid proteins in case of naked
   nucleocapsid viruses.
b) Some viruses have multiple serotypes.
c) Viral serotype is usually determined by the outer envelope proteins in case of
   enveloped viruses.
d) All the options are correct.

## MICRO-F-093
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: a
basis: Ch. 11 — Viral Structure and Classification {#ch11-5}
evidence: "Viroids: consist only of a single molecule of circular RNA without a protein coat or envelope; cause several plant diseases" — not animal tumors, which rules out (b); (c) describes defective viruses, a different item in the same atypical-agents list, not viroids.
note: Distinct from MICRO-F-066 ("A viroid is?", answer: naked infectious circular RNA molecule) — different stem and a different option set, kept separate.
seen: model 4

Viroids are atypical viruses that best described by which of the following sentence?
a) Consist of RNA without a protein or lipoprotein outer coat.
b) Cause tumors mainly in experimental animals.
c) Are defective viruses that are missing the DNA coding for the matrix protein.
d) All options are correct.

## MICRO-F-094
status: external
form: mcq
type: single
answer: a
basis: external — not covered in course material
evidence: The source lists tetanus toxin and botulinum toxin as distinct Gram-positive exotoxins without addressing cross-reactivity. Standard teaching: despite both being neurotoxins that block neurotransmitter release, they are immunologically distinct proteins, and antitoxin to one does not protect against the other.
seen: model 4

Antitoxin against tetanus protects against botulism as well, because the two toxins
share antigenic sites.
a) False
b) True

## MICRO-F-095
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
basis: Ch. 7 — Pathogenic Mechanisms {#ch07-7}
evidence: Lecithinase, hyaluronidase, and leukocidins are all described as directly damaging host tissue or cells. M protein is instead described as antiphagocytic (it blocks opsonization, preventing phagocytic killing) rather than a damage-causing factor — and the source attributes M protein to S. pyogenes, not S. pneumoniae, making option (d) doubly wrong.
seen: model 4

Which of the following enzymes (molecules) is not involve in causing host cell damage?
a) Lecithinase.
b) Hyaluronidase.
c) Leukocidin of Staphylococcus aureus.
d) M protein of Streptococcus pneumoniae.

## MICRO-F-096
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
basis: Ch. 13 — Viral Pathogenesis {#ch13-2}
evidence: "Viruses associated with cancer: ...hepatitis B virus (HBV) — liver cancer" — malignant transformation is the outcome the source links to chronic HBV infection.
seen: model 6

The following are possible outcomes of viral replication, which one is characteristic
of Hepatitis B?
a) Fusion of cells
b) Malignant transformation
c) No apparent or morphologic change
d) Lysis of cells (viral lysis)

## MICRO-F-097
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
basis: Ch. 12 — Viral Replication {#ch12-6}
evidence: In the lysogenic cycle, "the host cell copies its own DNA and the virus DNA, reproducing normally but passing virus DNA to all its offspring" — the prophage replicates as part of the host's own replication, not independently. The statement is False.
note: Near-duplicate theme of MICRO-F-002 (both about lysogeny/lysogenic conversion) but this is a plain True/False fact statement rather than an EXCEPT-style four-option question, and it was never answered here to compare against — kept separate rather than merged.
seen: model 6

In lysogenic transduction, viral genes replicate independently of bacterial genes.
a) True
b) False

## MICRO-F-098
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: c
claimed: d
basis: Ch. 7 — Pathogenic Mechanisms {#ch07-8}
evidence: "Exotoxins: polypeptides released by the cell" (not part of the cell wall) while "Endotoxins: lipopolysaccharides (LPS) that form an integral part of the Gram-negative cell wall" — this is the reverse of what option (d) claims. Exotoxins instead bind "specific receptors on the human cell membrane" via their B subunit, supporting (c).
note: The circulating file marks (d). The source directly contradicts it — being part of the cell wall is explicitly an endotoxin property, not an exotoxin one, making (d) backwards as written.
seen: Exam A (Dr. Dina Bitar, Second Hour Exam, Jan 2009) — "Microbiology 3"

Each of the following statements concerning exotoxins is wrong except:
a) They are less potent (ie, less active on a weight basis) than endotoxins
b) They are more stable on heating than endotoxins
c) They bind to specific cell receptors, whereas endotoxins do not
d) They are part of the bacterial cell wall, whereas endotoxins are not

## MICRO-F-099
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
claimed: a
basis: Ch. 7 — Pathogenic Mechanisms {#ch07-8}
evidence: "Exotoxins are polypeptides able to induce synthesis of protective antibodies (as in botulism and tetanus)" — antitoxins (IgG/IgM) are the described host defense against exotoxins; the source never describes macrophage-secreted proteases in this role.
note: The circulating file marks (a). The source documents antibody-mediated neutralization as the defense against exotoxins instead.
seen: Exam A — "Microbiology 3"

The main host defense against bacterial exotoxins is:
a) Activated macrophages secreting proteases
b) IgG and IgM antibodies
c) Helper T cells
d) Modulation of host cell receptors in response to the toxin

## MICRO-F-100
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
basis: Ch. 9 — Antibiotics {#ch09-2}, {#ch09-4}, {#ch09-5}
evidence: Aminoglycosides (streptomycin) are classified under protein synthesis inhibitors (30S subunit); mRNA synthesis inhibition is instead rifampin's mechanism. Streptomycin does not inhibit mRNA synthesis.
seen: Exam A — "Microbiology 3"

Each of the following statements concerning the mechanism of action of
antimicrobial drugs is correct except:
a) Bacitracin acts by inhibiting peptidoglycan synthesis
b) Naladixic acid acts by inhibiting the DNA gyrase of bacteria
c) Erythromycin is a bacteriostatic drug that inhibits protein synthesis
d) Streptomycin is a bactericidal drug that inhibits mRNA synthesis

status: external
form: mcq
type: single
answer: c
basis: external — not covered in course material
evidence: Standard teaching: the coagulase test distinguishes coagulase-positive S. aureus from coagulase-negative S. epidermidis. Ch.7 names S. aureus as coagulase-producing but the source never describes a coagulase test or its diagnostic use against S. epidermidis.
seen: Exam A — "Microbiology 3"

status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
basis: Ch. 7 — Pathogenic Mechanisms {#ch07-4}, {#ch07-8}
evidence: Clostridium tetani disease is driven entirely by its exotoxin ("single determinant of virulence: toxin"); protection against a purely toxin-mediated disease is achieved by neutralizing that toxin (the toxoid/antitoxin logic).

immunization is
a) To opsonize the pathogen (Clostridium tetani)
b) To prevent growth of the pathogen

form: mcq

form: mcq

form: mcq

form: mcq

answer: b

evidence: Stool microscopy distinguishes "an invasive rather than toxigenic organism." form: mcq

answer: a

evidence: "clindamycin can suppress the predominant normal flora, allowing a rare toxin-producing Clostridium difficile to overgrow and cause severe colitis" — the same antibiotic-induced flora-disruption principle applies to ampicillin, a broad-spectrum antibiotic.

## MICRO-F-108
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
basis: Ch. 7 — Pathogenic Mechanisms {#ch07-8}
evidence: Scarlet fever (erythrogenic exotoxin), toxic shock syndrome (TSST), and botulism (botulinum toxin) are all named exotoxin-mediated diseases in the source. Salmonella typhi (typhoid fever) does not appear among the source's exotoxin-producing organisms; its pathogenesis is invasion-based.
note: Already resolved from disputed to (b) at the extraction stage — two further captures of this exam showed an unambiguous single circled answer. The source confirms that resolution.
note (Job A): The original "Microbiology 3" photo showed what looked like two competing oval marks (one on b, one on c) and was recorded disputed on that basis. Two more captures of the same physical exam found in part 2 show an unambiguous single answer — (b) circled, (c) struck through as eliminated — so this is resolved to (b) rather than left disputed.

evidence: The source confirms exotoxin as diphtheria's "major virulence factor" and toxoid vaccination for prevention. Diphtheria is a localized nasopharyngeal infection where the toxin, not bacteremia, causes systemic effects, so blood cultures are not usually positive — standard teaching.
seen: Exam C, p3

The following are true about diphtheria, EXCEPT:
a) Exotoxin is a major virulence factor responsible for symptoms
b) The most common site of primary infection is the naso-pharynx
c) Toxoid is effective for prevention
d) Blood culture is usually positive

## MICRO-F-277
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
basis: Ch. 11 — Viral Structure and Classification {#ch11-4}
evidence: "measles virus has one serotype" — explicitly singular, unlike rhinovirus (100+), poliovirus (three), and HIV (constantly mutating, multiple), making measles the one that does not evade host defenses via multiple serotypes.

## MICRO-F-278
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: c
basis: Ch. 13 — Viral Pathogenesis {#ch13-2}, {#ch13-5}
evidence: The source explicitly attributes hepatitis A and B pathogenesis to immune-mediated (cytotoxic T-cell) killing, ruling out (b) and (d) as exceptions, and separately describes poliovirus as directly and lytically killing motor neurons through viral replication itself — a direct cytopathic mechanism, not immune-mediated — supporting (c).
note: Disputed source (large margin letter: c, poliomyelitis; small in-text mark: a, measles). Per the margin-outranks-inline tie-break, and independently confirmed by the source's own mechanism descriptions, the margin claim (c) is correct.

Immune mediated pathogenesis (immune lysis) is seen in all of the following
viral infections except:

evidence: Immunogenicity is defined as the capacity to induce an immune response, distinct from antigenicity (the capacity to react with existing antibody) — standard immunology.

## MICRO-F-329
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
basis: Ch. 11 — Viral Structure and Classification {#ch11-3}, {#ch11-4}
evidence: A capsomere is a capsid subunit, not "the complete unit of capsid and nucleic acid" (that is the nucleocapsid) — ruling out (a). The envelope derives from the nuclear membrane in herpesviruses, not exclusively the plasma membrane — ruling out (b). The nucleocapsid (genome + capsid proteins) is the universal core structure of every virus, supporting (d) (and closely related (c)).
note: Options (c) and (d) both align with the source's nucleocapsid framing; (d) is the claimed and retained answer since no source basis distinguishes them further.
note (Job A): This source's own numbering runs its own independent 1-60 sequence unrelated to every other exam in this file; cited here as "Q1" etc. purely as a locator within this one document, not as a cross-reference.

false EXCEPT:
a) The complete unit of capsid and nucleic acid is termed the capsomere
b) Viral envelope is a lipoprotein derived only from the plasma membrane of
   infected cells
c) A typical virion must have a nucleocapsid
d) The basic structure of all viruses is also known as the nucleocapsid

## MICRO-F-330
status: verified
form: mcq
type: single
answer: c
basis: Ch. 11 — Viral Structure and Classification {#ch11-3}, {#ch11-4}
evidence: Host specificity for naked viruses comes from capsid proteins, but for enveloped viruses it comes from envelope glycoproteins, not capsid — ruling out the "all viruses" claims in (a) and (b) (peplomers by definition belong only to enveloped viruses). The capsid (nucleocapsid) is explicitly essential to virion structure, supporting (c). Neutralizing antibody against an enveloped virus typically targets envelope glycoproteins, not the internal capsid — weighing against (d).
seen: Exam E, Q2

All of the following statements about functions of viral components are
false EXCEPT
a) Capsid proteins of all viruses determine host specificity and induce
   neutralizing antibodies
b) Peplomers of all viruses including non-enveloped viruses determine host
   specificity and induce neutralizing antibodies
c) The capsid is essential in the formation of virions
d) Mixing a suspension of enveloped viruses with specific antibodies against
   the viral capsid renders the virus noninfectious

## MICRO-F-393
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: a
claimed: d
basis: Ch. 16 — Parasitology {#ch16-6}; general parasitology (Echinococcus's definitive host is the dog, not humans)
evidence: The source explicitly states "the definitive host [of Toxoplasma gondii] is the domestic cat," not humans — ruling out (c) and (d). Echinococcus granulosus's definitive host is similarly the dog (standard parasitology; the source calls it the "dog tapeworm"), with humans as an accidental intermediate host (hydatid cysts) — ruling out (c) as well. By elimination, and because Giardia's life cycle occurs entirely within a single host with no distinct intermediate stage, it best fits the question's intent among these four.
note: The circulating file marks (d). The source directly contradicts the Toxoplasma component of that answer.
seen: Exam H, Q10

Human being is considered as a definitive host for which of the following
parasites?
a) Giardia lamblia
b) Toxoplasma gondii
c) Echinococcus granulosus
d) Both Echinococcus granulosus and Toxoplasma gondii

## MICRO-F-396
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: c
claimed: b
basis: Ch. 2 — Bacterial Cell — Structure and Function {#ch02-9}; Ch. 15 — Introduction to Mycology {#ch15-3}
evidence: Bacterial endospores (as in Clostridium tetani) are explicitly a survival/dormancy structure with "no metabolic activity," not a reproductive mechanism — one cell forms one spore with no increase in cell number. Fungal spores, by contrast, are described specifically as how fungi "reproduce" (sexual and asexual spore types). Grouping C. tetani with fungi as organisms that "reproduce by spore production" conflates a survival structure with true reproduction.
note: The circulating file marks (b). The source's framing of bacterial spores as purely survival structures argues against including C. tetani.
seen: Exam H, Q13

d) Clostridium tetani

## MICRO-F-419
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
claimed: c
basis: external — not covered in course material
evidence: Standard pharmacology teaching: penicillin "tolerance" (as distinct from resistance) in S. aureus is specifically attributed to failure of autolytic enzymes to activate after PBP inhibition — cell wall synthesis is blocked but the autolysin-mediated lysis step doesn't follow, so the organism survives without being genuinely resistant. Altered PBP is instead the mechanism of true resistance (e.g., MRSA), a different phenomenon from tolerance.
note: The circulating file marks (c). The question specifically asks about "tolerance," which standard pharmacology attributes to defective autolysin activation, not PBP alteration.
seen: Exam I

Some strains of S. aureus are tolerant to the action of penicillin because?
a) Autolytic enzymes are activated
b) Production of beta lactamase
c) Changes of penicillin-binding proteins
d) Autolytic enzymes are not activated

## MICRO-F-534
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d
claimed: b
basis: Ch. 12 — Viral Replication {#ch12-6}
evidence: The source describes specialized transduction as incorporating a specific, predictable nearby gene due to the phage's fixed integration site: "part of the phage DNA and part of the bacterial DNA, including the adjacent gal gene, are excised together" — a consistent, specific gene each time, matching "highly specific portion... regularly incorporated." Generalized transduction instead packages random host DNA fragments, the opposite of "highly specific."
note: The circulating file marks (b), generalized transduction — the mechanism that randomly incorporates DNA. Specialized transduction (d) is the one that consistently transfers a specific nearby gene.
seen: Exam J, Q76

What is the process of genetic recombination in which a highly specific
portion of the host gene is regularly incorporated into a virus?
a) Conjugation
b) Generalized Transduction
c) Transformation
d) Specialized Transduction

## MICRO-F-603
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: b
claimed: a
basis: Ch. 13 — Viral Pathogenesis {#ch13-6}
evidence: The source's documented immune-evasion mechanisms are cytokine decoys (viral proteins binding host cytokines like IL-1/TNF), reduced MHC class I expression, and multiple antigenic serotypes — none of which involve a virus synthesizing "human antibody neutralizing agents." Viruses do not produce antibodies; this statement misdescribes the evasion mechanism.
note: The circulating file marks (a), True. The source's actual immune-evasion mechanisms don't match this description.
seen: Exam K, Part Two (True/False) Q16

Viruses can evade the immune response by synthesis of human antibody
neutralizing agents.
a) True
b) False

## MICRO-F-608
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: a (Active immunity)
claimed: c
basis: general immunology (vaccine administration = active immunity by definition)
evidence: Administering a vaccine (even live-attenuated, like measles) stimulates the recipient's own adaptive immune response — the definition of active immunity. "Passive-active" combined immunization refers specifically to giving both antibody AND vaccine together (e.g., rabies or hepatitis B post-exposure prophylaxis), not simple vaccine administration alone.
note: The circulating file marks (c). Administering the measles vaccine alone is a textbook example of active immunity, not a combined regimen.
note (Job A): Part of a shared 3-item option list matched against Q1-5.
seen: Exam K, Part Three (Match) Q1

Administration of vaccine against measles
a) Active immunity
b) Passive immunity
c) Passive-Active immunity

note: Part of the same shared option list as MICRO-F-608.
seen: Exam K, Part Three (Match) Q2

## MICRO-F-627
status: conflict
form: mcq
type: single
answer: d (Amikacin)
claimed: a
basis: Ch. 9 — Antibiotics {#ch09-3}, {#ch09-4}
evidence: Vancomycin is explicitly a Gram-positive-only glycopeptide, never active against Gram-negative bacteria. Amikacin (an aminoglycoside) is explicitly used "to kill Gram-negative bacteria such as Pseudomonas... E. coli... Proteus... Klebsiella... Serratia," and as a non-beta-lactam, it is inherently unaffected by penicillinases.
note: The circulating file marks (a), Vancomycin — a Gram-positive-only drug, the opposite of "active against gram-negative bacteria."
note (Job A): Part of the same shared option list as MICRO-F-623.
seen: Exam K, Part Three (Match) Q20

Very active against gram negative bacteria & resistant to
penicillinases
a) Vancomycin
b) First generation cephalosporins
c) Fourth generation cephalosporins
d) Amikacin
e) Rifampin
f) Clindamycin
g) Sulfonamides
h) D + F
i) D + G
j) B + C

## MICRO-F-630
status: verified
form: mcq
answer: c
basis: Ch. 2 — Bacterial Cell — Structure and Function {#ch02-3}
evidence: The source's table of bacteria not visible by Gram stain gives "No cell wall; very small" as the reason for Mycoplasma pneumoniae specifically — not "a waxy coat" (that reason is given for Mycobacteria, a different organism). E. coli's thin peptidoglycan (pink) and S. pyogenes's thick peptidoglycan (blue) both match the source's comparison table, and "Gram positive bacteria phagocytosed by polymorphs will appear as gram negative" appears verbatim in the source.
note: Same unidentified background exam as MICRO-F-628.
seen: Exam L, Q9

Each of the following statements concerning the Gram stain is correct
except:
a) Escherichia coli stains pink because it has a thin peptidoglycan
   layer
b) Streptococcus pyogenes stains blue because it has a thick
   peptidoglycan layer
c) Mycoplasma pneumoniae is not visible in the Gram's stain because it
   does have a waxy coat
d) Gram positive bacteria phagocytosed by polymorphs will appear as
   gram negative.
